<summary>This is the title page of a posthumously published devotional work by the influential German mystic Karl von Eckartshausen, presenting moral tales intended for a sensitive, spiritually-minded audience. The text frames Christ as a dual archetype of heroic strength and patient endurance, offering these narratives as a virtuous alternative to secular novels.</summary>

# Preface.

The sudden death of the author prevented the earlier publication of the present book, which is the continuation of the already well-known first part, *Christ Among Men*. Every effort will be made to locate the remaining relevant manuscripts in order to fulfill what the deceased <note>The original German uses the term "der Selige" (the blessed one), a respectful way to refer to a person who has recently passed away.</note> (namely, the history of the early Christians presented through instructive narratives) promised to deliver. On this occasion, it should be noted that the noble deceased did not deserve the passionate criticism that someone took the trouble to insert into Lange’s *Ascetic Library* <note>This likely refers to "Langes Asketische Bibliothek," a series of theological and devotional writings edited by Samuel Gottlieb Lange in the late 18th century.</note>. He so honestly indicated in his preface the source from which he drew his material; it is therefore incomprehensible why he was treated so harshly instead of receiving thanks. On the contrary, it should [be] to the one (Eckart's

<summary>This preface explains the delay in publishing the sequel to "Christ Among Men" due to the author's death and defends the late author against harsh reviews in a contemporary theological journal.</summary>
<keywords>preface, posthumous publication, Christ Among Men, early Christians, Samuel Gottlieb Lange, literary criticism, Eckart</keywords>

hausen’s friends (one of whom is writing down this remark) would by no means fail to find much more to criticize in the presented depictions of the *Spirit of Jesus* *), which the reviewer believes has been so greatly dishonored by Eckartshausen's adaptation. To say nothing of <term>Socinianism</term> <note>Socinianism was a rationalist Christian movement that denied the Trinity and the divinity of Christ; in the 18th century, critics often used the term as an insult to describe any theology they felt was too focused on human reason rather than divine mystery.</note>, which prevails throughout the aforementioned book, *The Spirit of Jesus*: the style is harsh and cumbersome, unnecessarily wordy, filled with tedious repetitions, and burdened with every imaginable rhetorical ornament; furthermore, nearly every page is peppered with barbaric, modern-philosophical terminology. The following, in which Eckartshausen's own serene genius breathes, may prove the difference between original inspiration and mere copying.

## Jesus against His Betrayer.

---

O! how man’s natural instincts lead to his ruin as soon as they are misdirected, or lose their mutual balance! Even the drive for possession <note>original: "Eigenthum", referring here to the instinct for property or ownership</note> has already made many a person miserable; when it degenerates, then avarice and greed are its poisonous branches. 

The greedy man no longer views earthly goods as a means, but as an end in themselves; all his thoughts, inclinations, and intentions are fixed upon them. Whatever he does is calculated beforehand for his advantage; whatever he undertakes is for his profit. It is no longer he himself who determines his path, but self-interest <term>Eigennutz: a focused pursuit of personal gain, often implying a loss of moral agency</term> that sweeps him away to every action without further reflection. 

What a dreadful slavery for a human being! Yet, through this very thing, he also debases himself into the most wretched slave of other men. They need only [appeal to] his

<summary>This opening page provides a moral meditation on greed and avarice, describing how the pursuit of wealth can enslave the human will and lead to spiritual ruin. It serves as a philosophical introduction to the character of Judas Iscariot and his eventual betrayal of Jesus.</summary>
<keywords>Jesus, betrayal, greed, avarice, self-interest, moral philosophy, slavery of the soul</keywords>

--- Page 14 ---
<meta>This page continues the thought from page 1, completing the sentence regarding how others manipulate a greedy person by appealing to their vices.</meta>

<page-num>— 2 —</page-num>

give nourishment to his vice, need only satisfy his desires for the possession of earthly goods, and behold, he allows himself to be steered according to their will. Here again is the most shameful slavery on God’s earth, because its chains are worn voluntarily! — Just as greed <note>original: "Habsucht", a strong term for avarice or the "sickness of having"</note>, as a degenerated instinct, increases in inner strength, in that same measure the remaining instincts of the heart lose their influence. Gratitude, love, friendship, a sense of honor, and moral sense are therefore unable to move the greedy person toward what is noble, great, and for the common good. In a case of conflict <term>Collisions-Falle: a situation where moral duties or personal interests clash, forcing a choice</term>, he acts instead ungratefully, lovelessly, hostilely, shamefully, and against all morality, if only he can thereby gain temporal advantages for his person.

Now he is only one step away from crime. Some favorable circumstances align, and that step is also taken. The criminal stands there, and before he realizes it, his ruin is complete. While he was working toward it, he did not notice; now he is terrified by himself and by the sad consequences. This is, in brief, the moral history of a Judas,

<summary>This page describes the psychological and moral decay caused by greed, explaining how it overrides virtues like love and honor. It concludes by identifying this process as the "moral history" of Judas Iscariot, framing his betrayal as the result of a gradual loss of agency.</summary>
<keywords>Judas Iscariot, greed, moral decay, slavery of the will, vice, betrayal, ethics, psychological ruin</keywords>

--- Page 15 ---
<meta>This page continues the sentence from page 2, which introduced the story of Judas Iscariot as a "moral history" or psychological study of a man's descent into vice.</meta>

<page-num>3</page-num>

as a warning to posterity and as a mirror for thousands, in which they might see themselves. Jesus, the friend of humanity and a profound knower of the human heart <note>original: "Menschenkenner"—a term describing someone with deep psychological insight into human nature</note>, certainly attempted everything to save this unfortunate disciple. To him, the treasury <term>Cassa: the communal money-box or purse held by the disciples</term> was entrusted, from which not only the needs of the company were paid, but many poor people were also supported. There he had the best opportunity to accustom himself to necessary expenditures, to learn contentment <note>original: "Genügsamkeit", the virtue of being satisfied with what one has</note>, to gain the wholesome experience of how little a human truly needs, and especially to taste the divine joys of doing good. This was a visible proof of Jesus's foresight and wisdom, as he intended to prevent Judas's failing and, through constant habit, to encourage his gradual progress in virtue. However, Judas made a poor application of this; out of self-interest and greed, he stole from the treasury, and with every repeated act of this kind, he strengthened his flaw until it became a dominant vice. Jesus did not then fail to provide hints, exhortations, and warnings.

> "Beware," he once said, "of greed, for no one's life <note>This is a reference to the Gospel of Luke 12:15: "Take care, and be on your guard against all covetousness, for one's life does not consist in the abundance of his possessions."</note>

<summary>This page argues that Jesus intentionally gave Judas charge of the disciples' money-box as a therapeutic measure to teach him virtue and charity, but Judas instead used the opportunity to indulge his greed.</summary>

consists in the abundance of his possessions. <note>original: "davon, daß Er viel Güter hat." But in general, the excellent example of the Teacher unceasingly put the degenerate disciple to shame. Who acted more disinterestedly than Jesus? Who sacrificed more of His own advantages than He? Who conducted Himself more magnanimously than He on those occasions where splendor, prestige, power, and treasures were to be gained? Who practiced stricter self-denial than He, in order to prefer every duty over personal inclination? — To have such an example before one's eyes daily, to be constantly in such a school of living morality, and yet to remain unimproved, reveals a heart entirely without feeling for virtue and moral beauty. Sad effect of vice <term>Laster: a deeply ingrained moral flaw or wicked habit</term>! Whoever is once ruled by it has a sense only for that which [serves] vice

--- Page 17 ---
<meta>This page completes the sentence from page 4, which discussed how a person ruled by vice only recognizes things that nourish that vice. It then moves into a defense of Jesus’s decision to keep Judas as a disciple despite his flaws.</meta>

<page-num>5</page-num>

resembles it, is favorable to it, and provides it with new nourishment. One might ask: why did Jesus not dismiss the incorrigible <term>unverbesserlich: beyond correction or reform</term> disciple in time and expel him from the company? This question is easy to raise, but not so easy to answer. We know too little of the specific circumstances and the entire outward condition of the disciple to determine what the wisest Teacher should or should not have done. It is more correct for us to conclude that what the Teacher actually did was the best course of action under the circumstances. 

Jesus acted according to His character as a gentle, sparing, and compassionate friend of humanity <term>Menschenfreund: literally a "friend of humans," implying a deeply philanthropic and kind nature</term>, who did not wish to cast someone out, but rather to tirelessly improve them; not to embitter, but to shame them into better behavior; not to cause a deeper fall, but to preserve them from it; not to wound through harshness, but to guide through gentleness <term>Sanftmuth: a mild, patient, and meek disposition</term>. 

As an exile from the company, Judas would have fallen into despair just as surely as he later did as a traitor, because then all his hopes of gaining significant advantages in the kingdom of the Messiah <term>Messias: the promised deliverer; the text implies Judas viewed this kingdom in material or political terms</term> would have come to an end all at once. And should Jesus have caused a person's misfortune through His own fault? — No.

<summary>The author explains that Jesus kept Judas as a disciple out of compassion and a desire to reform him, rather than casting him out and potentially driving him to early despair.</summary>
<keywords>Jesus, Judas Iscariot, Messiah, compassion, gentleness, mercy, reform, discipleship</keywords>

--- Page 18 ---
<meta>This page continues the theological defense of Jesus's treatment of Judas, arguing that Jesus’s patience mirrors God's own relationship with humanity, and begins describing the events of the Last Supper.</meta>

<page-num>6</page-num>

He did not act so inconsistently. Just as God spares the immoral man, bears him with goodness, and applies every means for his improvement without robbing him of his freedom of will <term>Freyheit des Willens: the theological concept that humans choose their own actions</term>, nor working against his resolutions with force, so also did Jesus act as a visible image of the spiritual, invisible God. He kept the disciple in His company and sought to move him until the last day of his life through warning, gentle forbearance, friendship, and love, while leaving the rest to Providence <term>Vorsehung: the protective care of God as a spiritual power</term> with full confidence. In this regard, too, that solemn meal <note>The Last Supper</note> must be remarkable to us. *)

At the table, Jesus became sorrowful and said: "One among you will betray me. I indeed meet such an end as my destiny <term>Bestimmung: a person's divine purpose or calling</term> brings with it. But woe to that man who allows himself to be used as a traitor against me! It would have been better for him never to have been born." — He expressed Himself in the same way during the washing of the feet. "You are clean, but not all of you. He who ate my bread kicks me with his feet <note>original: "tritt mich unter die Füße"; this is a reference to the prophecy in Psalm 41:9</note>." — One

*) Matthew 26:21.

<meta>This page continues the narrative of the Last Supper, specifically focusing on the moment Jesus identifies Judas as His betrayer and the psychological state of the unrepentant disciple.</meta>

<page-num>7</page-num>

One can easily imagine the consternation into which the disciples fell when they heard that a traitor was in their midst. They searched their hearts and asked with a good conscience, "Is it I?" — one after another. Finally, Judas too had to ask, and he did so with the impudence that vice usually likes to have as a companion. Jesus answered according to the truth: "Yes, you are my betrayer!" — and immediately added: "What you are going to do, do quickly!" <note>John 13:27</note> in order to spare him embarrassment over it. 

If only the degenerate disciple had any moral feeling left! He took part in the meal of love <note>The Last Supper</note>, and in the act of friendship during the washing of the feet. He heard a language that could have penetrated his heart, a language that alerted him to his own misery and to the sad consequences of his resolution. He saw the sorrow of the friend of humanity <term>Menschenfreund: a term frequently used in this period to describe Jesus’s deep love and philanthropy toward all of mankind</term>, who had never offended him, but had always treated him with kindness, and even in this hour treated him with tender forbearance. But none of this made an impression on him. His resolution remained unchangeable toward the best, noblest person, who did not treat him with pre- <note>The text cuts off here; likely "prejudice" or "reproach"</note>

<page-num>8</page-num>

reproaches, nor sought to dismiss him with insults, contempt, or hatred, but still sought to win him over with forbearance, gentleness, mercy, and friendship—to betray Him for the worthless price of 30 pieces of silver <note>original: *Silberlinge*</note> (15 thalers) <note>The thaler was a standard silver coin used throughout German-speaking lands from the 16th to the 19th century; the author provides this conversion to help his contemporary audience grasp the smallness of the bribe</note>. How such examples must *humble* us! — and at the same time *outrage* us against that accursed *vice* <term>Laster: a habitual or repeated sin that corrupts character and moral standing</term>, which suffocates all gentle feelings, blunts all moral sense, and terribly hardens the heart against all natural impulses of *humanity*! How can one still hesitate for a moment to swear irreconcilable *hatred* to *vice* and to *abhor* it in the highest degree? However, the unfortunate person who is thereby shamed, degraded, and made inhuman is to be pitied and deserves our *compassion*. For this is how the *Deity* <term>Gottheit: a common Enlightenment-era term for God, emphasizing His divine nature and sovereign character</term> Himself acts. He cannot hate the human being as His creature, but He can indeed hate the *vice* within the person. *Jesus* acted no differently toward *Judas*, and we must admire Him in this *divine greatness* all the more, the more intimately *vice* intertwines itself with the whole person, giving him the appearance

<summary>The author reflects on the tragedy of Judas’s betrayal for a small sum of money, arguing that while we should hate the vice that corrupts a person, we should feel compassion for the person themselves, following the divine example set by Jesus.</summary>

He was the one whom the disciple, greedy for 30 pieces of silver <note>original: *Silberlinge*</note>, intended to betray. Nevertheless, in His mind, Jesus separated the vice from the person; He pitied the latter as an unfortunate victim and honored the **human dignity** <term>Menschenwürde: the inherent worth belonging to every human being, a central concept in Enlightenment ethics</term> within him with heartfelt forbearance. In this way, the wretched man was meant to feel that he was making not the object of his betrayal, but himself, most miserable. --- Page 23 ---
<meta>This page continues the sentence from page 10, describing Jesus's gentle manner when predicting the disciples' abandonment.</meta>

<page-num>11</page-num>

do, without making bitter reproaches to them about it. It is once again entirely the language of love, friendship, and leniency. They are meant to feel their own weakness all the more vividly, while simultaneously learning to see that nothing unexpected is happening to their Teacher, so that they might find peace again more quickly after the event. The Resurrection and the journey to Galilee still lay before them—how much comfort there was for them later in these refreshing words! The sublime Sage <note>original: *erhabene Weise*; the author frequently uses titles like "Sage" or "Wise One" to describe Jesus, reflecting an Enlightenment-era emphasis on His moral teachings and intellect.</note>, who well knows the impact of the following scenes of horror upon their spirits, does not intend to hold against their hearts what happens merely out of human weakness. He thus forgives them before they fail; He reconciles Himself with them before they abandon Him.

— But Peter, with his vehement temperament, begins: “Lord, even if all the others should fall away from You, I would never do it. No! I shall never desert You!” So little does man know himself; so little does he know what he will decide an hour from now, or how he will act on the following day! Deeply, very deeply, even an otherwise good person can sink when certain circumstances...

sig I

unite, or unexpected temptations entice him. To prevent these circumstances, or to remove these temptations, is seldom within his power. He can only restrict and master the impression they make upon his soul <note>The German "Gemüth" refers to a person’s inner disposition, encompassing their emotional state and moral character.</note>. But commonly, a person forgets himself there without reflection; a lack of deliberation allows him to follow his passions in that moment, and behold—a single step away from the path of reason <note>original: "Vernunft"</note> and duty leads him into unforeseen transgressions. When he finally comes to his senses again, he stands like someone who has been led blindly to a completely foreign place, only to have the blindfold suddenly torn from his eyes. He is astonished at how it was possible for him to go so far astray. He feels painfully humbled by his own self-debasement, and the most sensitive shame remains in his heart for a long time. Blessed is he if he uses the misstep <note>original: "Fehltritt"</note> in the way that it can be used! It is in this alone that the good man distinguishes himself from the bad. Both fail; but the good man becomes all the more cautious in future cases, more attentive to himself and to all circumstances, and consequently stronger in his pursuit of the good.

<meta>This page continues from the previous discussion of Peter's predicted denial. It moves from the dialogue itself to a psychological analysis of why Peter failed and an exploration of Jesus’s pedagogical wisdom in preparing Peter for his eventual moral recovery.</meta>

page-num 14

Peter cannot understand this at all, especially now while his courage is so great and the danger is still far off. He does not believe himself capable of being able to deny his Lord. And yet, it is precisely this lack of self-knowledge <note>original: "Selbsterkenntniß"</note> and this false confidence in his own heart that form the basis for his upcoming misstep. 

Had he been less self-assured, he would have been more attentive to himself and his circumstances, rather than recklessly exposing himself to such a powerful temptation. Nevertheless, this incident also serves to illustrate the spirit of Jesus in all its moral excellence <note>original: "moralischen Vortrefflichkeit" — This term reflects the Enlightenment-era focus on Jesus as a supreme moral teacher and example of ethical perfection.</note>. 

Gently, He reminds His over-confident yet fundamentally weak disciple <note>original: "Schüler" — literally "student" or "pupil," emphasizing the role of Jesus as a teacher.</note> of the danger. Full of forbearance, He forgives him his guilt even before Peter’s own heart feels the weight of it. With wisdom, He uses the yet-to-be-committed mistake to improve the one who errs—and even to improve others, who are meant to be strengthened in goodness through this example. Carefully, He linked the reminder of the truth of His words to a minor circumstance—the crowing of a rooster <note>The "rooster-crow" (Hahnengeschrey) is used here as a psychological trigger designed to awaken Peter's conscience at the exact moment of his failure.</note>—so that even when Peter could no longer hear the voice of his Lord <note>The text ends mid-word with "verneh-", likely completing as "vernehmen" (hear/perceive) on the following page.</note>

<meta>This page concludes the sentence from the previous page, describing how the soul, having been prepared through earthly trials, is now ready for its transition. capable, through the infinitely faster progress in <term>virtue: "Tugend," the practice of moral excellence</term> and holiness, of becoming ever more blessed beings in the <term>Kingdom of God: "Reiche Gottes," the spiritual realm of divine governance and eternal life</term>.

Under the preceding assumptions, the aforementioned question—why did Jesus have to suffer so extraordinarily?—is now easy to answer. First, He Himself, as the definite ideal of virtue, could not have been elevated to such moral perfection other than through suffering. Through this, He attained that moral height and dignity for which the world still admires Him after millennia. And precisely that terrifyingly full measure of His supreme sufferings was the reason why His <term>greatness of spirit: "Geistesgröße," a nobility of soul and character</term> could distinguish itself all the more, and every facet of His sublime, excellent character could come to light more clearly. Insofar as a higher degree of virtue, proven through suffering, is necessarily connected with a higher receptivity for pure divine joys, so too must Jesus have subsequently gained a fuller measure of <term>supersensory bliss: "übersinnlicher Seligkeit," a state of spiritual happiness that exists beyond the physical world</term> in accordance with His merit. Secondly: He was destined to live for humanity, and [to give] this life for humanity—

<summary>The author explains that Jesus's extreme suffering was necessary to demonstrate His perfect virtue and spiritual greatness, which in turn allowed Him to attain the highest level of divine bliss. This serves as a model for how suffering elevates the soul toward moral perfection.</summary>

### 6.

## Jesus Mistreated Among the Crudest of Men.

---

When violent storms in the dark of night tear the clouds asunder and clear the gloomy sky, look—then the full moon shimmers in its mild light. The nightly storms roar, but it remains in calm majesty. Clouds rush past to darken its beautiful face, but sublime in quiet greatness, it softens the nightly black darkness through its radiance. Chased further away, every bank of clouds soon vanishes further down the horizon, but the moon floats gently and undisturbed in its full glory until its path is completed. — Yet where is there an image in all of nature with which the suffering Sage <term>der duldende Weise</term> <note>The author uses "Sage" or "Wise One" to describe Jesus, a common Enlightenment-era phrasing that emphasizes his moral philosophy and intellectual composure during suffering.</note> could be compared? He who, in the midst of the thunderstorm of misfortune, let the heavenly light of his virtue <term>Tugend</term> shine, and in quiet greatness of soul <term>Seelengröße</term> <note>This term refers to "magnanimity" or a "lofty soul"—the ability to remain dignified and unshaken by external circumstances or insults.</note> [upheld] the dignity of the [humanity] with <note>The sentence ends mid-phrase as the page concludes; it likely continues to describe the dignity of human nature even when faced with cruelty.</note>

<summary>This page opens Section 6 with an extended metaphor comparing Jesus’s calm endurance during his trial to a moon remaining majestic despite a raging night storm. It emphasizes his "greatness of soul" and virtue in the face of misfortune.</summary>

<keywords>Jesus, storms, full moon, majesty, greatness of soul, virtue, misfortune, suffering sage</keywords>

[dignity of] humanity united with God even under the torments of death? After the condemnation, Jesus was abandoned to the mistreatment of depraved men. Court servants, attendants, maids, and soldiers, in whose crude hearts no moral feeling stirred, directed their mockery at him—though he had never offended them. He was handed over to their custody and exposed to their power until the further decision in the morning. Usually, such people abuse the appearance of their power in a most cruel way, and since they lack all finer feelings of humanity <term>Menschlichkeit</term> <note>In this context, "humanity" refers to the moral and empathetic qualities that define a civilized person, contrasting with the "brutality" of the guards.</note>, they degrade themselves to the point of brutality <term>Brutalität</term> in their treatment of the unfortunate. If they fully believe themselves entitled to such excesses by the example of their masters, then the outbursts of their crude hearts are without limits, and they abandon themselves to an unbridled freedom without the slightest restraint. Among people of this kind, Jesus—whose spirit deserved to be enthroned in the realm of moral beings—had to stand for a time, bound and condemned by the High Council <term>hoher Rath</term> <note>The Sanhedrin, the supreme religious and legal assembly in Jerusalem, which had just concluded its preliminary sentencing.</note>. Some spat in his face.

He could not have been indifferent, being a man of the finest moral sentiment, who recognized all the rights of humanity in their foundations, possessed the highest dignity of virtue <term>Tugend</term> himself, and combined the most sublime wisdom <term>Weisheit</term> with the purest goodness of heart. —and asked: “Are you the King of the Jews?” Jesus answered: “Do you say this of your own accord, or did others tell it to you about me?” — An important counter-question for the judge. A key concept in Enlightenment-era thought, referring to the natural human capacity for rational judgment, independent of dogma or external influence.</note> to recognize that a man of such appearance, in such a situation, and with such previous behavior could be no worldly king <note>German: *äußerlicher König*, literally an “external king.” The author suggests that Jesus lacked any of the political trappings or military ambitions of a typical ruler or revolutionary.</note>. He had to be convinced through his own rational reflection that the Jews—who were languishing for dominion—would hardly deliver up their own king unless they had other secret intentions. But if he saw through the eyes of others, judged according to the talk of others, and wished to decide based on false concepts from the mouths of others, then he was not in a position to fulfill the duty of justice as a judge. This is roughly what Jesus wanted to give him to understand, as delicately as it was done gently. Had he taken the hint, he would have seen, by comparing the accused with the accusers, on which side the truth lay. — Pilate asked further:

<meta>This page follows the discussion of Jesus's legal defense, transitioning from the legal perspective of his trial to the psychological and moral character of his accusers, the priests and Pharisees.</meta>

Jesus furthermore had to deal with enemies who let themselves be blindly governed by their passions and slavishly ruled by their vices. One understands the power of raging passions and the vehemence of vice far too little if one believes that they can ever be satisfied without the required sacrifice. The hatred of the priests, unleashed without limits, did not rest until the enlightener of the people <note>original: "Volksaufklärer"—this term reflects the 18th and 19th-century Enlightenment context of the author, portraying Jesus as a rational teacher who brings clarity to the masses.</note> had been removed from the way. 

And the bigoted Pharisees, offended in the pride of their hypocrisy, could forgive neither the virtue nor the reputation of the man who had dared to publicly expose the abomination of their hearts. He had to die to satiate their thirst for revenge, and indeed die ignominiously on the cross like a criminal slave. 

In such circumstances, should Jesus have defended himself in vain? No, he remained silent. In this way, he most strongly proved the firmness of his resolve to submit to the moral order of things under God's supervision, even with the greatest self-denial <term>Selbstverläugnung: the act of sacrificing one's own interests, safety, or desires for the sake of a higher moral duty or the well-being of others</term>, voluntarily sacrificing his life out of conviction for the tru- <note>The text cuts off here at the end of the page; it likely continues as "truth" (Wahrheit).</note>

so may world history <term>Weltgeschichte: the grand narrative of human events, often viewed in this period as a moral school for humanity</term> set forth another for us in his place. Oh! how venerable has misfortune already become through the persons who have since endured it! But among all, Jesus stands at the top. His heart convinces him of the nobility of pure intentions, with which he acted at all times as a loyal citizen <term>Staatsbürger: here used in an Enlightenment sense to describe Jesus as an exemplary member of his society</term>, as a noble patriot, and as a worshiper of virtue. He feels himself entirely worthy of reaping gratitude from his nation, which he sought to ennoble with tireless zeal. He can make the highest claims to applause, praise, and esteem, because he possesses the highest merits toward his fatherland and toward humanity. These claims of his are all the more just, as he always acted selflessly and with the noblest self-

denial of his powers, sacrificed himself for the common good <term>allgemeinen Besten: the "common good," a central pillar of Enlightenment ethics focused on the welfare of society</term>. His unstained love for humanity deserves the greatest, most heartfelt reciprocal love, and his virtue deserves public veneration. And behold! He is hated, insulted, reviled, portrayed as a criminal, treated as a worthless slave, and condemned to a shameful death with loud vehemence by a blinded mob. Where was there ever a misfortune in the world so great, so undeserved, so taxing for body and soul, so unique in its kind, as what Jesus had to suffer? And how did he conduct himself during this? Merely silent and patient in the quiet majesty of his character. Thus it was decided through him forever that divine virtue is more powerful than anything in the world, and can be overcome neither by terrors of the future nor by present torments.

<meta>This page continues the sentence from page 103, which discussed the anxiety Jesus felt during his spiritual struggle in the Garden of Gethsemane.</meta>

had to struggle there. If he now endured his pains with such high steadfastness <term>steadfastness: *Standhaftigkeit*, a core Enlightenment-era virtue referring to moral consistency and endurance under pressure</term>, it clearly happened out of deeply held principles. He did not want to diminish the virtue for which he bled through any whimpering or cries of lament, but rather to uphold its dignity even amidst physical pain through calm endurance. He did not want to desecrate himself through any unseemly gestures, as he was one who suffered under God’s eyes and according to God’s plan—indeed, he had voluntarily resolved to endure even the most difficult trials unto death.

Finally, he sought to properly master the natural sensations of humanity through an internal counter-force, thus maintaining the superiority of his spirit over the body. In doing so, he aimed to become a venerable model of self-sacrifice for his disciples. To them, he had once promised: “Be of good cheer! I have overcome the world.” <note>original: "Seyd getrost! ich habe die Welt überwunden." A direct quotation from the Gospel of John 16:33.</note> Could he have fulfilled this promise more faithfully than he did at this moment, when the power of his pain did not overcome him, but was instead overcome by his calm, persistent steadfastness?

When the Roman procurator <term>procurator: a high-ranking official in the Roman Empire, such as Pontius Pilate, who was responsible for the judicial and financial administration of a province</term> heard of the Son of God, he became even more anxious and had Jesus led back into the judgment hall. "Where are you from?" he asked him, and received no answer. What was the Noble One supposed to answer him? As a pagan, he had neither a proper concept of the Messiah nor any understanding of the moral sense of a "Son of God." <note>The author highlights the cultural gap between Pilate’s Roman polytheism and the Jewish/Christian theological concepts Jesus represented.</note> On the other hand, through his natural reason, he could well perceive that a man was no capital criminal <note>original: "Todesverbrecher," one whose crimes warrant the death penalty</note> merely for the confession of being a Son of God. Jesus therefore gave him no answer, being just as wise in his silence as he was far removed from flattery to win the favor of the judge. The latter then began: "What? — you do not speak to me? — Do you not know that I have the power to have you crucified, and the power to release you?" *) The author knows of no similar example of this kind from a pagan sage of antiquity. One would have to count that often-praised story of *Diogenes* <note>Diogenes of Sinope (c. 412–323 BCE), the Cynic philosopher who famously lived in a barrel and prioritized virtue over worldly power</note>, who had no favor to ask of King *Alexander* <note>Alexander the Great</note> other than that the king might step out of his sunlight <note>According to legend, when Alexander asked if he could do anything for the philosopher, Diogenes replied, "Stand out of my light," showing his disdain for worldly status</note>.

Therefore, the king, even if he had shimmered with purple and gold, had to stand before the messenger of God, who was brought forth in a simple form, and feel his own powerlessness! — Jesus’s speeches must become ever more important to us the closer we see him to the goal where he concludes his sacrificed life. They grant us the most beautiful glimpses into the interior of his heart, and make us completely acquainted with the most glorious spirit that [filled] him...

...filled him until the very end. Does not the ordinary person <term>ordinary person: *der gewöhnliche Mensch*, referring to someone without exceptional spiritual discipline</term> often change their opinions and maxims when they face death? And does there not exist a great difference even in a virtuous person regarding their feelings at the time when they still consider death distant, and in the hour when they see it, as it were, visibly before them? What they at first did not fear now makes them anxious, and their wavering principles often seem to want to desert them just where they need them most for their peace of mind. Not so Jesus. Calm, comforted, true to himself, he went toward his death—and indeed toward the violent death on the cross. He had reason to hesitate when he thought of the disheartening incidental circumstances; and see! nevertheless he remained true to his principles. His virtue was not shaken, his courage not weakened, his trust in the Divinity <note>original: *Gottheit*</note> not suppressed, his steadfastness not defeated. No anxious doubts tormented his pure heart in the decisive hour. From his words to the weeping women there emerges the deepest peace of his...

<meta>This page continues the sentence from page 132, which described Jesus facing the end of his earthly life.</meta>

he was to step off, and in spirit reach that place where our physical nature <term>physical nature: *Sinnlichkeit*, referring here to the sensory and bodily aspects of humanity that instinctively recoil from pain and death</term> shrinks back in horror. But he walked the dark path with firmness, looked calmly at the thick curtain beyond which death is viewed quite differently than it is here, and did not allow himself to be shaken in the least by any images of the imagination. This again allows us to infer the degree of his conviction—perhaps regarding immortality and eternal life? That as well, certainly, but primarily (and in this lies an even greater moral merit) regarding the beneficial consequences of his death for the spread of his teaching, for the blessedness of humanity. 

This is why, even amidst external terrors, his resolve did not waver—the resolve to sacrifice himself according to the recognized plan of the Divinity; why, instead, completely composed, he awaited the further course of events under higher guidance, without sighing over his pains, without complaining about the cruelty of his enemies, without trembling before the greater torments lying in wait. Earlier, he had tested the cup <note>original: *Kelch*; an allusion to the "Cup of Suffering" from the biblical account of Gethsemane</note>, and though he found it very bitter,

but found it wholesome for humanity. Now he was ready to drain it completely, even to the last drop. If that wise man <note>original: *jener Weise*; a reference to Socrates, the Greek philosopher who calmly drank hemlock after being sentenced to death</note> still offered up something from his poison cup, then Jesus offered himself up as the most beautiful moral sacrifice to the Divinity. Here we have once again an image of the highest morality of character <term>morality of character: *Sittlichkeit des Charakters*, a key concept in Enlightenment thought referring to an individual's internal commitment to ethical duty</term>. We cannot contemplate it enough, as it refreshes our heart with the most sublime consolation: that moral goodness is more powerful than anything terrible the earth has to show; and that moral freedom <term>moral freedom: *sittliche Freyheit*, the capacity of the human will to act according to reason and virtue despite physical pain or outward force</term> ultimately overcomes all attacks of external violence. In this sense as well, Jesus has stripped death of its power and shown that the moral spirit of man can and should be death’s master, not its slave.

## Jesus dies.

---

The final event of Jesus’s life on this side of the grave follows. It, too, is important and

<vocab>Jesus, Morality, Moral sacrifice, Freedom, Death, Moral spirit, Master</vocab>

--- Page 155 ---
<meta>This page continues the sentence from page 134, describing the final events of Jesus’s life as instructive and moving.</meta>

instructive, moving, and shattering, dejecting and refreshing, according to the various points of view from which one observes it. Above the cross, where he hung in the midst of evildoers, Pilate had a tablet fastened with the inscription in three languages <note>original: *in drey Sprachen*; referring to the Hebrew, Greek, and Latin inscription described in the Gospels</note>: “Jesus, the King of the Jews.” Actually, these words were intended to indicate the cause of his death. However, even in this, one cannot fail to recognize the secret plan of Providence <term>Providence: *Vorsehung*, the belief in a divine power or God guiding the events of the world toward a purposeful end</term>. A large part of the Jewish nation was thereby first made acquainted with an idea which, as new ideas were subsequently linked to it, was to promote the most beneficial revolution in people’s minds. At that time, it was only an idea of moral childhood <term>moral childhood: *moralische Kindheit*, a term used by Enlightenment thinkers to describe a stage of human history or individual development where morality is understood through simple, literal, or legalistic concepts</term>, such as the Jews could grasp for the time being. On the other hand, for the future, therein lay a seed of nobler concepts, which time gradually developed under changed circumstances. From the executed King of the Jews came a moral King of the World, whose kingdom, as the Kingdom of Truth and Virtue, was to encompass all nations.

<summary>This page reflects on the inscription "King of the Jews" placed on the cross, interpreting it as a providential catalyst that transformed a localized political title into a universal symbol of moral truth and virtue.</summary>
<keywords>Jesus, King of the Jews, Pilate, Providence, moral childhood, King of the World, Kingdom of Truth and Virtue, moral revolution</keywords>

Here, two extremes of our contemplation are always presented in the image: namely, the highest level of vice <note>original: *Lasterhaftigkeit*</note> in the crowds of blinded Jews, and the highest morality in the person of Jesus. The more the former fills us with loathing, the more gently the latter refreshes us with heartfelt pleasure. The choice of which side we should lean toward can no longer be difficult for us. Who could love a black hailstorm, pregnant with devastation, more than the setting evening sun departing in silent majesty?

## Jesus is taken down from the cross.

The sky was still covered with thick darkness. The trembling of the earth, which split the rocks, the terrors and the shudder which moved through all of nature, had gripped <margin>the</margin> crowds of spectators too deeply. Many people stood crowded around the cross; both nearby and in the distance. But priestly hatred <note>This refers to the religious leaders who opposed Jesus, portrayed here as being driven by an unrelenting animosity.</note> was least of all satisfied by this spectacle. Although Jesus had already surrendered his soul into the hands of his heavenly Father, they still wished to exact bloody revenge upon his holy corpse. This is the course of the most wicked vice <note>original: "Laster"</note>: even by the completion of the deed, the will of malice is not yet satisfied. The wicked man feels an ever hotter thirst: the more he devours, the more agonizing it becomes

<summary>This page describes the somber reaction of the Jewish people to the supernatural events following Jesus's death and introduces the narrative of his removal from the cross, highlighting the persistent hostility of his detractors.</summary>

<keywords>Crucifixion, Son of God, repentance, darkness, earthquake, priestly hatred, vice, malice</keywords>

--- Page 169 ---
<meta>This page continues the sentence from the previous page, detailing the insatiable nature of the malice directed toward Jesus by his accusers and their request to Pilate regarding the bodies on the cross.</meta>

for his desire to be satisfied. Thus the priests, the scribes, and the elders of the people—all these practical atheists <note>The author uses "practical atheists" to describe religious leaders who, despite their status, act as if God does not exist or does not see their malice.</note>—sent word to Pilate. "Today is the eve," they sent word to say, "the day of rest of our great Sabbath <note>original: "Sabbats"</note>. Only if we are purified to a high degree may we celebrate the Passover festival <note>original: "Paschafest"</note> tomorrow. Therefore, give the command so that the corpses of those cursed by God <note>A reference to the Mosaic Law (Deuteronomy 21:23) stating that anyone hanged on a tree is under God’s curse and must be buried the same day to avoid defiling the land.</note> do not remain hanging on the cross until tomorrow; let their legs be broken, and then let the bodies be taken away." 

We have here two features of the lowest malice of these deceivers of the people to consider. They had convinced themselves of the certain death of Jesus, and yet they vehemently demand that his legs also—though he was already dead—should be broken, and not only those of the two still-living murderers. This was a measure <note>original: "Verfügung"</note> which was undertaken merely to hasten death. Just as the entire course of Jesus’s condemnation was nothing but a passionate intrigue, so too did this act—since they made no exception for the dead Jesus—possess a character all the darker, as this breaking of legs was unnecessary for their ultimate goal, which they had already reached

had, and no longer served at all. — Then, to justify themselves, they again held up the shield of religion as their defense. Thus, priestly hatred and hypocrisy, through which the former is cultivated and nourished, are always the two most dangerous and passionate motives in the world: and as a wise man of antiquity says, the abuse of the best becomes the worst <note>original: "der Mißbrauch des besten wird auch das schlimmste." This is an English rendering of the Latin proverb *Corruptio optimi pessima*, suggesting that when something naturally good is corrupted, it becomes uniquely evil.</note>. Now the satisfying answer came from Pilate. — Soldiers stepped forward, swinging the bloody club with sinewy arms, and smashed them down so that Golgotha <note>original: "Golgata"</note> echoed, and dust rose all around the crosses. Thus they broke the bones of the first man, and also of the second. But when they came to Jesus and saw that he was already dead, they did not break his legs.

One of them, perhaps even more humanely, in order to quickly put an end to all further investigations <note>The "investigations" refers to the Roman practice of verifying that a crucified person was actually dead before removing them from the cross.</note>, seized a spear and pierced his side: immediately blood and water flowed down from the opened wound. Thus he, who had already been bleeding for hours for the welfare of all humanity, offered up even the last drop from his divine heart, which is the seat of love. The sublime deceased, whose entire life was spent doing good, though having passed away, still wished to bear witness that his death was a death of love—that he died for the people whom he loved so faithfully and tenderly until his end. 

His bitterest enemies could now demand no clearer testimony as to whether he truly were dead; likewise, for those few friends of Jesus who had held out until now (still hoping that God would take up the cause of this righteous man), the weak reed <note>original: "Rohrstab". With sighing breath, they climbed over the bones of the dead up the <term>Hill of the Dead</term> <note>original: "Todten-Hügel"; a reference to Golgotha, which means "Place of the Skull." Now both stopped at a modest distance from the crosses, looking with deep melancholy toward those who were even more sorrowful.

Standing like statues of stone beside the <term>stronger Maccabean woman</term> <note>original: "Machabäerinn"; a poetic comparison to the mother of the seven martyred sons in the biblical Books of Maccabees, used here to emphasize the inner strength and stoic grief of Mary, the mother of Jesus</note> were the disciple and several women gathered around the hanging deceased. Only Mary, the sister of <term>Lazarus</term>, visibly wrestled with nameless agony before the <term>Forgiver of Sins</term> <note>original: "Sündenvergeber"; a title for Jesus emphasizing his role in the redemption of humanity</note>. She, who loved so tenderly, called upon heaven and earth for compassion, and cried out to the Avenger for vengeance.

"Oh, let us offer them comfort," the men said, "if any comfort still remains in the midst of such misery."

They called out with the voice of friendship, "We have come to bury the holy deceased." They approached with trembling hearts those who were now awakened to a new <term>misery</term> <note>original: "Jammer"; deep lamentation or woe</note>. They took the <term>body</term> <note>original: "Leichnam"</note> of the lifeless one from the cross, lowering him gently onto the hill of <term>Golgotha</term>. Their eyes often turned away in grief, and their tears fell uncontrollably. They tended to him and anointed him. In the midst of this work, which was so pleasing to God, they were often forced to pause, in order to [master] their feel- <note>The text breaks off mid-word: "Empfin-" likely intended to be "Empfindungen" (feelings or emotions).</note>

### 12.
## Jesus is buried.

<image-desc>An ornamental typographical flourish divider featuring a central oval knot motif with horizontal lines tapering outwards to both sides.</image-desc>

Jesus was the greatest hero in battle, and the greatest endurer in suffering. If we review the entire life of this divine friend of humanity, what was it but a perpetual struggle against our common enemy, the passions, <note>In this 18th/19th-century context, "passions" refers to unruly emotions or worldly desires that lead one away from virtue.</note>

<summary>The author describes the total disillusionment of the disciples following the burial of Jesus, whose political hopes were crushed by the sealed tomb, before beginning a spiritual tribute to Jesus's life of endurance.</summary>

<meta>Continuing the final sentence from page 172, this page completes the thought that human passions act as a "common enemy" before transitioning into a devotional reflection on Jesus as the ultimate model for spiritual victory.</meta>

which storm against us like a countless army, unceasingly assailing us, and so often defeating our courage and our most resolute intentions. What was the life of our Savior <term>Heiland: Savior or Redeemer, a common title in German devotional literature</term> but a perpetual state of suffering <note>original: "Leiden"</note>. Jesus has left us the true example for combating these treacherous enemies, our passions <note>In the 18th and 19th centuries, "passions" referred to unruly emotions or worldly desires that were thought to lead the soul away from virtue.</note>. He has given us powerful and stirring examples of how to triumph over them. Indeed, the life story of Jesus contains, to the highest degree, everything that can be said on this subject. 

Through his story, we learn how we should approach the noblest of all battles—how we should suffer, fight, and conquer in order to liberate ourselves from the slavery of sin. All of Hell rose up against Jesus, yet the Son of Man <term>Son of Man: a title for Jesus (Menschensohn) that highlights both his shared humanity with us and his divine mission</term>, who took all our infirmities upon himself, remained ever constant in his greatness and majesty. Unchanging and unlike an earthly hero, he stood far removed from all cunning; victoriously, he cast the entire power of the world, the flesh <note>original: "Fleisch." In this context, "the flesh" represents human weakness and the temptation to prioritize physical comforts over spiritual duties.</note>, and Satan into the dust before him. — Let us briefly review the objects of this struggle, which is as significant as it is glorious.

"Can you believe?" was the first question of this divine Master before he opened the treasures of his wisdom to anyone. He has blasphemed God by saying he can forgive sins; he heals on the Sabbath; he wants to tear down the temple and rebuild it in three days; he does not respect Moses' law—and more such ambiguous, sophisticated speeches <note>The author is listing the specific biblical accusations brought against Jesus as examples of "sophistry" used to manipulate the public</note>, which confused the understanding of the common people, had they devised.

The temple servants mixed the true faith that Jesus demanded with their blind belief in miracles <term>Wunderglauben: a faith based solely on witnessing supernatural signs rather than internal moral transformation</term>; this was their brand of faith, in which they sought to maintain the people. Jesus, who had now already fulfilled all the duties of a divine teacher, performed no more miracles at this point. All the more easily did contempt and mockery find entry among the crudest class of people. These people, too, must have been bribed by the priests' hatred, through which they permitted themselves every kind of wantonness. ### Jesus before the Roman Judge —

The latter was a pagan <note>original: *Heide*, referring here to Pilate's status as a Roman official who did not follow the Jewish faith</note>. Just as every kind of malice was to be expected from the hatred of the Jews against Jesus, it must have brought some comfort to the followers of Jesus that his case was now being investigated before a court that took no part in the Jewish laws intended to condemn Jesus, nor in the intrigues of the accusers and their hypocrisy.
